# Nage v1 Specification

Status: draft 3, 2026-10-26, amended through milestone 1 by decisions 38 to 84. Written from the kickoff interview, amended after the planning interview, then revised after two independent reviews (a consistency review and a skeptical-implementer review) the same day. Section 2 records every decision and what was rejected, so later arguments can start from the record instead of from memory.

## 1. Summary

Nage is open-source (AGPL-3.0) personal accounting for one household. It runs as a single Rust binary on a home server, serves a browser UI that works on a phone, and keeps one set of books in one SQLite file. Under the hood it is a double-entry, cash-basis, single-currency ledger. On the surface it says "paid from Checking to Groceries" and never says "debit" or "credit".

The workflow v1 must nail: import bank and card CSV exports, categorize them with rules, and see where the money went. Everything else in this document is either in service of that workflow or small enough not to endanger it.

## 2. Decisions record

| # | Topic | Decision | Rejected alternatives |
|---|-------|----------|-----------------------|
| 1 | Audience | A shared household, several people on one set of books | Single user; built for strangers from day one |
| 2 | Must-nail workflow | Import, categorize, see where money went | Net worth over time; reconcile to the penny; replace a spreadsheet |
| 3 | Accounting model | Double-entry core, plain-language UI | Ledger-style UI with signed postings; single-entry with categories |
| 4 | Basis | Cash basis, credit cards as liability accounts | Accrual |
| 5 | Household shape | Home server with simple logins (chosen over the smaller "no logins" option; auth, sessions and concurrent writes are v1 work) | One install with no logins; device sync |
| 6 | Platform | Local web app, server-rendered | CLI; desktop app; TUI |
| 7 | Storage | Single SQLite file | Plain-text ledger; Postgres |
| 8 | Data sources | CSV import plus manual entry | OFX/QFX/QIF; direct bank connections |
| 9 | Currency | Single currency per book | Per-account currency; full multi-currency |
| 10 | Duplicate imports | Fingerprint per row plus a review queue for near-matches | Import everything and flag later; refuse overlapping date ranges |
| 11 | Categorization | User rules plus "same as last time" suggestions | Manual only; learned classifier |
| 12 | Reconciliation | Light: a statement-balance check with per-posting ticking and locking; no line-by-line statement matching and no statement import | None; full reconciliation sessions against imported statements |
| 13 | Reports | Register, income vs expense by category, balance sheet, month-over-month trends | Budget vs actual |
| 14 | Budgets | None in v1 | Simple monthly targets; envelope budgeting |
| 15 | Investments and assets | Balance-only accounts whose changes post to a valuation-changes equity account | Out entirely; lots, prices and cost basis |
| 16 | Extras in scope | Tags on transactions; phone-friendly layout | Recurring transactions; attachments |
| 17 | Stack | Rust, axum, sqlx, server-rendered HTML with htmx | Go; TypeScript; Python |
| 18 | Auth | Users with passwords, everyone sees everything, first user is admin | Roles; per-user private accounts; one shared password |
| 19 | History | Who/when stamps plus soft delete | Hard delete; append-only audit log |
| 20 | License | AGPL-3.0 | GPL-3.0; Apache-2.0; MIT |
| 21 | Transfers | Auto-match mirror rows across imports; transfers between household members are transfers, not income or expense | Import both legs and merge by hand; import one side only |
| 22 | Test fixtures | Real anonymized exports from the household's banks | Synthetic only |
| 23 | Fixture banks and currency | Major US and Canadian banks, Canadian first; the book currency is CAD | Synthetic fixtures; USD |
| 24 | Multi-account exports | A profile may name an account-selector column; one import run per account, the selector value chosen at import time; single-account exports leave it blank | One run fanning out to every mapped account; splitting files by hand |
| 25 | Reference deployment | The household server (x86_64, Ubuntu, Docker Compose): a Compose project on its service pattern, an amd64 image on GHCR, HTTPS through tailscale serve | arm64 targets; TLS inside Nage |
| 26 | Process | GitHub Actions on `main`; about eight milestones; one PR per milestone, CI green, reviewed and merged by the maintainer | PR per slice; commits straight to main; five or fifteen milestones |
| 27 | Ordering | Auth in milestone 2 before any ledger screen; import before manual entry; deployed to the household server from milestone 2 and redeployed each milestone | Dev auto-login; manual ledger first; deploy only at v1 |
| 28 | UI base (amended by 126 in PLAN M2 slice 6a) (provisional, maintainer to confirm) | A style file of Nage's own, `app.css`, written by hand on a small base of its own with no CSS framework underneath, naming the fonts Nage vendors beside htmx (46, 126); Playwright from milestone 1 | A vendored classless CSS framework under a small custom sheet (Pico CSS's classless build, the first choice, which left in PLAN M2 slice 6a: three prototypes built separately each spent a quarter to a third of their style file undoing it, and without it the pages carry 27 KB of style instead of 98 KB); Tailwind; browser tests later |
| 29 | Undo import | An import can be undone outright (superseded by row 32) | Soft-deleting the import's transactions, which would block a corrected re-import by fingerprint |
| 30 | Fix-release lane (amends 26) | Besides the milestone PRs, a `fix/<slug>` PR may land a regression fix found after a deploy, tagged `v0.N.x`; the PR's "how to try it" is a post-merge check on the server | Waiting for the next milestone; PR images built before merge |
| 31 | Browser matrix (amends 28) | Two Playwright projects: WebKit at phone size (390×844, the iPhone 13 profile) and Chromium at desktop size (1280×800); the household's phones are iPhones | Four projects (both browsers at both sizes) |
| 32 | Undo semantics (amends 29) | Undo removes an import outright whenever none of its postings is reconciled and no later import references it; edits, categorizations, tags and soft deletes made since the import are counted on the confirmation page and lost with it | Refusing undo after any edit, which left a bad import stuck until manual delete existed |
| 33 | Currency immutability | The currency is chosen at setup (a select defaulting to CAD) and never changes afterwards; a wrong choice means starting the data directory over before entering data | Editable until the first posting |
| 34 | Transfers deferred wholesale (amends 27) | Milestone 3 lets rules and inline picks target income and expense accounts only; nothing creates a transfer leg until mirror matching exists in milestone 5 | Creating transfer legs in milestone 3 that nothing could match, which double-counted every transfer |
| 35 | Security headers | A Content Security Policy with no inline scripts or styles, `Referrer-Policy: same-origin`, and htmx configured with eval disabled, from milestone 2 | Adding headers at the end |
| 36 | Dependency checks | `cargo deny` license check blocks merges; the advisory check runs on a schedule and does not block unrelated PRs | Advisories blocking every PR; no dependency checks |
| 37 | Scheduled snapshots | None inside Nage; consistent backups are the on-demand `VACUUM INTO` plus the host's backup taking a consistent copy by path | A nightly in-process snapshot with retention |
| 38 | License variant (amends 20) | `AGPL-3.0-or-later` is the SPDX expression in Cargo.toml and the README; the LICENSE file is the unchanged AGPL-3.0 text | `AGPL-3.0-only` |
| 39 | Float lint level (amends INV-3) | `#![forbid(clippy::float_arithmetic)]` in `nage-core`, so no `#[allow]` inside the crate can reopen it | `deny`, which a local `#[allow]` overrides with the pipeline still green |
| 40 | Security headers timing and scope (amends 35) | The three headers of 5.3 are set verbatim on every response (HTML, JSON, static, 404, errors) by one middleware from milestone 1 slice 2, and the base layout carries the htmx meta config with `allowEval` and `includeIndicatorStyles` off from the same slice; milestone 2 slice 6's e2e run asserts no CSP violations in the browser console with htmx loaded, and each later htmx interaction is covered by the slice that builds it; hyper's own answer to a malformed request (400, 414 or 431), sent before the router runs, carries none of these headers and gets no response log event, only axum's `trace`-level connection error (a known gap) | Headers from milestone 2 as decision 35 reads; HTML responses only |
| 41 | Health probe before storage (amends 5.2) | `/health` answers through a probe held in the app state; milestone 1 slice 2 ships an always-ok probe, so a server without storage answers 200, and proves the 503 path with a failing probe; slice 3 replaces the probe with the book-row count | A hard-coded 200 with no tested 503 path; always 503 until storage exists |
| 42 | Configuration surface (clarifies 5.2) | Flags are named after their variables: `serve` takes `--data-dir`, `--bind`, `--tz`, `--log`; `health` takes `--bind`; `--trust-proxy` arrives with its consumer in milestone 2. The binary resolves flag over variable over default itself; an empty variable counts as unset; a variable that is not valid UTF-8 is a configuration error, except `NO_COLOR`, whose value is never read as text: any non-empty value turns colour off (decision 47); every configuration error names its source (`--bind`, `NAGE_BIND`, `TZ`) and the value; only the `nage` binary reads the environment, `nage-web` is handed values | Global flags on every subcommand; clap's `env` feature (an empty variable is an error and messages name the flag only); parsing `NAGE_TRUST_PROXY` before anything reads it; a configuration file |
| 43 | Time zone resolution (amends 5.2) | `NAGE_TZ`, else the `TZ` variable when `NAGE_TZ` is unset, else UTC; the value used must be a name known to the time-zone database (legacy names such as `EST5EDT` included, `Etc/Unknown` excluded); anything else (an unknown name, a POSIX rule string, a file path) refuses to start naming the variable it came from; an empty variable counts as unset; `/etc/localtime` is never read. jiff carries `tzdb-bundle-always` from milestone 1 slice 2, with the host zoneinfo preferred when present | Falling back to UTC on an invalid `TZ`; `TimeZone::system()`; bundling only in the Docker slice |
| 44 | nage health (amends 5.2; amended by 157 in PLAN M2 slice 8) | Reads only the bind address; 0.0.0.0 maps to 127.0.0.1 and :: to ::1; two seconds is one total deadline over connect, request and status line; exit 0 on 200 and 1 for any other outcome (another status, connection refused, timeout); an invalid or port-0 bind address is a configuration error and exits 2 like every subcommand (reachable only by misconfiguration, so a healthy container never shows Docker's reserved code 2); silent on success; on failure the three labelled lines of 157 on stderr naming the target URL, the system's own words in one pair of brackets (from PLAN M2 slice 8; until then one line); the body is never parsed; hyper's client over a TCP stream, already in the tree, no TLS | Validating the data directory or time zone; ureq or reqwest; a hand-written HTTP request; printing the body; one line on stderr (PLAN M2 slice 8, 157) |
| 45 | Bind address, exit codes, startup and shutdown (clarifies 5.2) | `NAGE_BIND` is an IP address and port, IPv4 or bracketed IPv6, no hostnames; port 0 is allowed for `serve`, which logs the actual address. `serve` logs one start-up event with version, bind address, absolute data directory, time zone with its source and log filter before binding, installs the SIGTERM and SIGINT handlers before logging the listening line, and on either signal stops accepting, finishes in-flight requests without a deadline and exits 0. Exit codes for every subcommand: 0 success; 2 usage and configuration errors; 1 runtime failures | Hostnames resolved by DNS; a separate port variable; all failures exit 1; one code per failure kind; a shutdown deadline |
| 46 | Vendored assets and static URLs (clarifies decision 28 and SPEC 11; amended by 125 and 126 in PLAN M2 slice 6a) (provisional, maintainer to confirm) | htmx stays on the 2.x line (the newest `v2.0.x` tag at vendoring time; htmx 4 is a separate decision because 5.3 and 5.4 use 2.x configuration names), minified as distributed, and the four IBM Plex files of 126, the maker's woff2 files unchanged, under `crates/nage-web/static/vendor/` with their upstream license files and a `VENDORED.md` naming version, tag, tag commit, source URL, cross-check, SHA-256 and byte count; the icons of 125, drawn for Nage, under `crates/nage-web/static/icons/`; every served file listed once in nage-web's list of served files, its content type given by the extension of the name it is served under; embedded with `include_bytes!` and hashed with `sha2`; served at `/static/<stem>.<hash>.<ext>` where the stem is the file name before its last extension and the hash the first 16 hex characters of the content's SHA-256, with `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable`; every other path under `/static/` is 404. The fonts' licence text travels inside the program (126): `LICENSE-ibm-plex` keeps its name on disk and is served under the name `LICENSE-ibm-plex.txt`, so at `/static/LICENSE-ibm-plex.<hash>.txt`, as plain text (`text/plain`), and no page links it. Since an address changes only when the file's bytes change, the style file names each font at its address literally, and a test holds those addresses and the style file together, a wrong address being a silent fallback font | Query-string versions; serving unhashed names; htmx 4 now; `rust-embed` before the asset count needs it; the fluid build; Pico CSS 2's `pico.classless.min.css` (vendored until PLAN M2 slice 6a, 126); the fonts' licence text kept in the repository only (the program carries the fonts, and the Open Font License asks that its text travel with every copy of them) |
| 47 | Logging (amends 5.2; amended by 160 in PLAN M2 slice 8) | Text on stderr, ANSI only when stderr is a terminal and NO_COLOR is unset; NAGE_LOG is a tracing filter directive defaulting to info (a bare level is valid; an invalid directive refuses to start; RUST_LOG is ignored); one event per line, a whole-second UTC stamp YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SSZ from a timer of Nage's own, the level, the message and the fields as key=value, no module name, every path in a field shown as 157 shows it so that an event stays one line (from PLAN M2 slice 8, 160; until then the subscriber's RFC 3339 UTC with sub-second precision and the module name); one event per response with method, path without the query string, status and latency_ms, written by hand-written middleware, at info, except that a /health response with a 2xx status is logged at debug so a passing container health check stays out of the default logs while a failing one shows; the user id joins the event with sessions in milestone 2; log stamps are not stored timestamps | stdout; JSON logs; tower-http TraceLayer (logs the full URI); every /health response at info; every /health response at debug; a level-only grammar; sub-second stamps and the module name (PLAN M2 slice 8, 160) |
| 48 | Licence allowlist (clarifies decision 36) | `deny.toml` admits permissive licences as the dependency tree needs them (MIT, Apache-2.0, BSD-2-Clause, BSD-3-Clause, ISC, Zlib, 0BSD, Unicode-3.0, Unlicense, MIT-0, CC0-1.0), one line per licence with the first crate that needed it; copyleft or unusual terms need a maintainer decision recorded in `deny.toml`, never an addition in passing; vendored web assets keep their upstream license files in the tree | A complete allowlist written up front; asking per line; silent additions |
| 49 | Slice tracking (amends PLAN 1.1) | A milestone's slice lines get GFM task markers when the milestone starts, ticked in the commit that finishes each slice (milestone 1's slice 1 is ticked after the fact); a slice is done when its tests pass, the local checks in the README's Commands table are green and it is independently reviewed; once CI (milestone 1 slice 5) and the e2e projects (slice 7) exist, done also means its tests run in CI and its screen passes the phone check that applies to it, gates discharged for the earlier slices when those two slices land | A progress note per milestone; git history only |
| 50 | Templates and axum (clarifies SPEC 11) | askama templates render to a `String` and return axum's `Html`; a failed render logs the error and answers 500; no integration crate | `askama_web`; `askama_axum` (deprecated) |
| 51 | Dependency pinning | Every external crate is declared once in `[workspace.dependencies]` with a caret version, `default-features = false` where the defaults are not wanted, and the features every user needs; a member adds further features in its own manifest; the committed `Cargo.lock` is the pin | Exact `=` pins; per-crate versions |
| 52 | Test citations (clarifies SPEC 9 and 10.1) | Every test's doc comment cites the invariant it proves or, where none applies, the narrowest SPEC clause or decision it proves, or the PLAN slice line when SPEC is silent; invariant ids are cited only by tests that prove them | Citing a touched but unproven invariant; no citation |
| 53 | Storage pools and busy (clarifies 5.4) | Storage lives in nage-core: a writer pool of exactly one connection and a read pool of four read-only connections over `data/nage.sqlite`; every connection carries `foreign_keys=ON` and `busy_timeout=5000`, the writer also sets `journal_mode=WAL` (persistent, set before the readers open) and `synchronous=NORMAL`; the only way to write is `Storage::begin_write`, which issues `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`; a request waits up to 30 s for the writer connection and 5 s for a reader; "busy" means SQLite answered SQLITE_BUSY (primary code 5, after its 5 s) or a queue wait expired, the error names which, and the request gets 503 with `Retry-After: 5` and the busy page, the cause logged at `debug`; any other storage error is a 500 after an `error` log (the decision 50 pattern); slice 3 proves the classification, the lock and the page, the HTTP path is proved with the first POST route (M2 slice 5); at shutdown the read pool closes first and the writer last, so the last connection checkpoints and removes the WAL; sqlx's statement logs stay at `debug`, statements slower than 1 s and a connection wait longer than 2 s at `warn` (a write queued behind a long import is meant to show), and sqlx's logger records no bound values | All waits 5 s (a form during a five-second import would fail; SPEC 10.3 needs two parallel imports to both commit); no in-process timeout; `BEGIN DEFERRED`; silencing the slow-acquire warning |
| 54 | Start-up with storage (clarifies 45; amended by 155 and 160 in PLAN M2 slice 8) | After the start-up line serve takes the data folder's in-use mark (153), then opens the storage and brings it up to date through the one operation of 155 (the writer first, creating nage.sqlite if missing and setting WAL, then the read-only readers; the backup of 151 when a change waits; every pending change all or nothing), logs backup written and older backup removed when it wrote and removed copies, then one database ready event with the path, the number applied and the schema version, runs the check in one read transaction and logs it (160), then makes the dummy hash, installs the signal handlers, binds and logs the listening line, so no request is answered before the schema is current and the book checked; a failure to take the mark, open, back up or update exits 1 with the three lines of 157 naming the file or the change; a database that carries a change unknown to the binary refuses to start with a message saying a newer Nage wrote it and naming the newest backup this version can open (152); before the handlers exist a signal takes the default action and SQLite rolls the unfinished update back | Handlers before migrating; binding first and answering 503 while migrating; the check after listening (PLAN M2 slice 8, 160) |
| 55 | Health definition (amends 41) | `/health` runs `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM book` on the read pool through the probe with a one-second deadline: 200 whenever the query succeeds, whatever the count (a fresh database with no book row is healthy), 503 on any error or timeout, with one `warn` line; `HealthProbe` stays the mechanism and `HealthProbe::always` stays for tests of other routes | 503 without a book row; a probe without a deadline |
| 56 | Schema conventions (clarifies 7, INV-5) | Every table is `STRICT`; booleans are INTEGER 0 or 1 with a CHECK; every date column carries `GLOB` plus `date(d,'+0 days') IS d`, every timestamp column `GLOB` plus `strftime('%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ', t, '+0 seconds') IS t` (whole seconds, `Z`); `book` is `id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY CHECK (id = 1)`, `name TEXT NOT NULL`, `currency TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (currency GLOB '[A-Z][A-Z][A-Z]')`, `minor_digits INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (minor_digits BETWEEN 0 AND 3)`, `created_at` with the timestamp CHECK; INV-5's "set once" is enforced by core (no trigger), the schema enforces the singleton, the shapes and NOT NULL; `book` gains `updated_by`/`updated_at` by `ADD COLUMN`, each with its CHECK, with W14 (M8) so 5.1 holds for the name edit, and 5.5's finality rule reads per column ("a column's constraints are final from the column's creation"); the book row is created before any user exists and carries no `created_by`; "Types are SQLite affinities" in 7 reads as the STRICT storage classes; a STRICT database needs SQLite 3.37 or newer to open, which the bundled library and any current `sqlite3` CLI satisfy | Plain affinities; a format CHECK on dates only; the minimal pair of checks |
| 57 | sqlx line and query workflow (clarifies 11 and PLAN 1.2; amended by 155 in PLAN M2 slice 8) | sqlx 0.9 with runtime-tokio, sqlite-bundled, macros, migrate and no TLS; sqlx-cli at the same minor (pacman -S sqlx-cli on Arch, else cargo install sqlx-cli@<version> --no-default-features --features sqlite --locked); the committed .env holds only DATABASE_URL=sqlite:target/dev.sqlite and SQLX_OFFLINE=true, never a secret, so every ordinary build reads the committed workspace-root .sqlx; after a query change run cargo sqlx prepare --workspace -- --all-targets (it goes live by itself; the dev database comes from cargo build then cargo sqlx database setup, and cargo sqlx migrate run after a migration change); cargo sqlx prepare --check --workspace -- --all-targets joins the README commands run before every commit; sqlx::migrate!("../../migrations") lives in nage-core with a build.rs rerun line; sqlx's _sqlx_migrations table is outside SPEC 7: its default timestamp is exempt from 5.4, Nage only reads it, and W15's JSON export leaves it out; from PLAN M2 slice 8 every pending migration runs inside one outer BEGIN IMMEDIATE begun through Storage::begin_write in Storage::migrate, the one update path that serve, nage migrate, nage init, cargo xtask seed and the tests all take, sqlx's migrator nesting each migration in a savepoint, so an update is all or nothing (155) and 5.4's exemption of sqlx's plain BEGIN is gone; whether the bookkeeping table exists is asked by a checked query on sqlite_schema, never by sqlx's ensure_migrations_table, which creates it; unchecked SQL (a literal, or a statement assembled from test constants and marked AssertSqlSafe) is allowed for PRAGMAs, VACUUM INTO (the backup's path bound as a parameter, never assembled into the statement, 151), migration bookkeeping, transaction control (BEGIN IMMEDIATE) and connection-mode tests, and for schema and corruption tests, never for building scenario databases | Live-by-default builds (PLAN's text); .sqlx per crate; SQLX_OFFLINE only in CI and the Dockerfile; each migration in its own plain BEGIN (a failure part way left the earlier migrations applied, PLAN M2 slice 8) |
| 58 | Milestone fixtures and test support (clarifies PLAN 1.3 item 5 and M1 slice 4; amended by 161 in PLAN M2 slice 8) | cargo xtask seed --out <path> creates a database through nage-core's storage, migrates it, writes the current milestone's made-up household through core operations alone (from PLAN M2 slice 8, 161; until then it wrote a freshly migrated, empty book) and closes it cleanly (no -wal or -shm left; a failed migration or seed leaves the partial file, which the error message names); slice 3 committed fixtures/books/v0.1.0.sqlite, frozen with 0001_init.sql since the v0.1.0 tag and no longer reproducible by the seed; PLAN M2 slice 8 commits fixtures/books/v0.2.0.sqlite, written by the seed and written again whenever the seed changes until the v0.2.0 tag, then frozen; a test opens a copy of a committed book, never the file itself, and the migration test loops over every book under fixtures/books (94, 161); *.sqlite is marked binary in .gitattributes; tempfile is the dev-dependency for temporary files from slice 3 | Producing the fixture in slice 4 or by hand; hand-rolled temporary directories; a seed of several scenarios chosen by a flag (the frozen books are the history) |
| 59 | Test support (amends 58 and PLAN M1 slice 4, clarifies SPEC 10.1 and 11) | Shared test helpers live in nage-core behind the `test-support` feature as `nage_core::test_support` (`crate::test_support` in nage-core's own unit tests under `src/`; nage-core's `tests/` use `nage_core::test_support` like every other crate), enabled only by dev-dependencies (nage-web, nage and xtask list `nage-core = { workspace = true, features = ["test-support"] }` under `[dev-dependencies]`, nage-core lists itself the same way), so a production build never carries it (`cargo build -p nage` and the Dockerfile build without `--all-targets` or `--all-features`); the feature only adds the module and must stay additive, because every `--all-targets` build unifies it into the whole workspace; `tempfile` and `proptest` are optional normal dependencies of nage-core under that feature, declared once in the workspace table, and no `[dev-dependencies]` table names them today (row 58's "the dev-dependency" reads accordingly; a crate other than nage-core adds `proptest.workspace = true` to its own `[dev-dependencies]` with its first property, row 61). The module holds `TestDir` (a `tempfile` directory with a canonical path, removed on drop, kept when it is dropped during a panic), `fixture_copy` (copies `fixtures/books/v<version>.sqlite` into a fresh `TestDir`; the committed file is never opened), the storage openers (`open_fresh`, `open_migrated`, `open_with`, `migrate`), the side-file assertion and `proptest_config`; each crate's `tests/common` keeps only crate-local helpers. Process-level tests bind port 0 and keep what they bind: a `serve` child reports its address in the listening line, and an in-process listener (`HealthServer`, `BlackHole`, an occupied port) is held for the test's duration and read with `local_addr` (the tests of the default bind address are the exception: they use 127.0.0.1:8787 and accept either outcome); a test that needs a port where nothing listens holds a bound, non-listening socket (`ClosedPort`) for its duration. The test tooling's own variables (`PROPTEST_*`, `INSTA_*`, `CI`, `TMPDIR` and the test runner's own) are read by the harness at test time and are outside decision 42 | A separate test-support crate (a dev-dependency cycle; one more package; a database-backed helper is unusable from in-crate unit tests); per-crate copies; hand-rolled temporary directories; a released port read and dropped before use |

| 77 | The fixture guard (clarifies PLAN section 6, 10.1, 10.4, 11, 42, 57, 67 and 71; amends PLAN M1 slice 8's scan scope: every file under `fixtures/` by type, with a layout check) (provisional, maintainer to confirm) | `scripts/fixture-guard.sh` is a bash script in the smoke script's style (GNU grep with PCRE, find, sort, od, tr, sed, iconv, jq and the `sqlite3` command-line tool; `LC_ALL=C.UTF-8`, so digit classes are ASCII-only and case folding covers accented letters); `scripts/fixture-guard-selftest.sh` proves the rules its checks name on synthetic fixtures in a temporary directory, one numbered check per case naming what it proves. Without arguments the guard scans the repository's `fixtures/` with the layout check on; `--fixtures-dir DIR` scans another directory the same way (a symbolic link resolved to its target); with paths it scans the given files or directories with the layout check and the path-name scan off (candidate fixtures outside the repository: a file that is neither JSON nor a book is read as a CSV, and the list is always required). Rules: every path under `fixtures/` fits decision 76's layout and each `csv/` directory holds `sample.csv`, `profile.json` and `expected.json` (a stray file, a `-wal` side file, a misnamed book, an uppercase or hyphenless directory name, a symbolic link fail). A CSV: a UTF-16 byte-order mark or any NUL byte fails (W3 refuses UTF-16; a byte scan cannot see through either); the digit rule runs on the file's bytes; for the deny-list scan a file that is not valid UTF-8 is decoded from Windows-1252 as a whole (W3's reading, except that W3 reads a file starting with a UTF-8 byte-order mark as UTF-8), and one that iconv cannot decode, because it holds one of the five bytes glibc's table leaves undefined (0x81, 0x8D, 0x8F, 0x90, 0x9D), fails as neither UTF-8 nor Windows-1252 (stricter than W3, whose decoder maps them). The digit rule: a maximal run of seven or more ASCII digits fails unless it starts with `999` (the reserved prefix), is exactly eight digits reading as a date from 19000101 to 20991231 with month 01-12 and day 01-31, or is the integer part of an amount, followed by a period and one to three digits; one `grep -P` pattern (`DIGIT_RE` in the script: a lookbehind that refuses a preceding digit, lookaheads that refuse `999`, the date shape and the amount shape, and a possessive `[0-9]{7,}+` that keeps the engine from backtracking into a shorter run); a decimal comma is never exempt (at line level `…,1234567,12,…` would read as an amount); stated blind spots: a number written in groups split by any non-digit (spaces, hyphens, periods, slashes) and anything under seven digits (the deny list's job). A JSON file: every string value (a repeated key's values included, through jq's streaming parser) and every object key of the parsed document is scanned by both rules, every number by the deny list alone; a file jq cannot parse fails (an empty file passes). A book: the file is copied into the guard's scratch directory and the copy opened with `sqlite3 -readonly` (the committed file is never opened, decision 58); every ordinary table but SQLite's own `sqlite_*` is read column by column (a virtual table, its shadow tables and a stored generated column are not; the schema defines none), TEXT values only (INTEGER amounts and BLOB checksums are never scanned), each value on one line with its line breaks read as spaces and nothing quoted, excluding a value of 32 or more hexadecimal characters (fingerprints and file, configuration and completion hashes, which hold a seven-digit run with probability 0.6) and a value starting with `$argon2` in any letter case (password hashes); a TEXT value holding a NUL byte fails (the tool would cut it off); a file with no table or that sqlite3 cannot open fails. Every path name under `fixtures/` is scanned against the deny list, and against nothing else (a number used as an alias passes unless it is listed), and so is every file by its kind; `README.md` and files of other kinds get the deny-list scan only. The deny list comes from the environment variable `FIXTURE_DENY_LIST` (decision 78): one entry per line, trimmed, blank lines and lines starting with `#` ignored, a trailing CR stripped, an entry under two characters a configuration error, a list that loads no entry counting as missing; an entry is matched as a fixed string without regard to case, as a whole word (letters, digits and underscore are word characters, so `example.com` matches inside `a@example.com` while `cedar` does not match `cedarwood`), or, when it is all digits, at digit boundaries wherever it stands (`C456789` is a hit, `14567890` is not). Output: one `scanned` line per file, one `FAIL <place>: <hint>` line per problem, the deny-list state (`deny-list entries: N`, or the notice that the list is unset, empty or holds no entry), and a last line `OK, N file(s) scanned` or `FAILED, M problem(s) in N file(s) scanned`; a digit run is reported as its place with the run's length and first three digits, a deny-list hit as its place with the entry's line number in the list, never the entry, the run or the cell (a path that matches an entry is necessarily named, and its own annotation carries no `file=` property, since GitHub masks a secret in a message, not in a property; other problems under that path still name it); the places are `<file>:<line>` for a CSV or text line, `<file>: JSON string N` (the Nth string value in document order, then the keys, each object's in sorted order) or `JSON number N`, and `<file>: <table>.<column> (value N)` (the Nth TEXT value read, tables in name order, columns in order, exempt and NUL-bearing values not counted); a problem with a whole file names the file alone, a layout problem the path, a column holding NUL-bearing TEXT values `<file>: <table>.<column>` with their count, and a missing list `deny list`; under GitHub Actions each problem is also an `::error` annotation (property values percent-escaped) and the skipped deny-list scan a `::notice`. Exit 0 when nothing was found, 1 on any problem (a missing list with a fixture present and a path argument that does not exist included), 2 on a usage error, a missing tool or grep without PCRE, a `--fixtures-dir` that is not a directory, a temporary directory the guard cannot create, a CSV, a book or (with a list) a text file the guard cannot read (an unreadable JSON file is reported as not valid JSON), or a short entry. The self-test's 14 checks: a conforming layout passes, through a symbolic link too, no side file left beside its WAL-mode book; the notice without a list and without a CSV fixture, the layout check and the digit scan still running; the failure without a list with a fixture, the digit scan still running; a list of blanks and comments counts as missing, a one-character entry is an error and a two-character entry is accepted; the digit rule on both sides of each exemption (a six-digit run, `9991234567` and `9990123456` against `9981234567`, `20260102` and `19991231` against `20261345`, `20261301`, `20260132`, `20260100`, `20260002`, `202601021`, `18991231` and `21000101`, `1234567.89`, `123456789.5` and `1234567.123` against `1234567,89`, `12345678.1234` and `1234567.`, `A1234567`, `$ 1,234,567.89`, and the `FAILED` summary line with its count); UTF-16 and NUL; the Windows-1252 decode with a digit run on the same line, and a byte neither UTF-8 nor Windows-1252; deny matching as fixed strings (`exampleXcom` is no hit), whole words, any case, a padded entry trimmed, digit boundaries for an all-digit entry (a following digit blocks a hit; check 5's `123456789.5` pins a preceding one), `README.md` by the deny list alone even on a line holding an encoding error, with the entry number and never the entry in the output; JSON strings and keys scanned by both rules (a repeated key's values, nested values and nested keys included, a line break read as a space), numbers by the deny list, nothing matched printed, a file jq cannot parse failed; a book's TEXT values scanned, INTEGER and BLOB cells not, hash and argon2 values exempt and a 32-hex value too, a ten-digit value, a long value that is not a hash and a value merely starting with `$` scanned, an apostrophe and a line break inside a value matched, a vertical bar inside a value never shown, a NUL value failed, a WITHOUT ROWID table read, SQLite's own tables skipped (a planted `sqlite_sequence` row); an empty book and a file that is not a database; the layout (a stray file, inside a fixture directory too, a side file, a misnamed book, an uppercase and a hyphenless directory, a symbolic link, a missing required file including `sample.csv`, `rules.json` allowed, a path name on the list); argument mode (a `.TXT` candidate read as a CSV, a `.JSON` one as JSON, a missing path a problem, an empty argument too, a `--fixtures-dir` that is not a directory and a temporary directory the guard cannot create exit 2); the annotations (a text line's with `file=` and `line=`, a comma in the property percent-escaped, a JSON string hit's without `line=`, a path hit's without `file=`). A guard rule and a self-test check are tests in decision 67's sense: a CI fix never loosens or removes one without the maintainer. Decision 57 and 10.1's scenario-database sentence read with the guard's read-only scans and the self-test's synthetic books (built with the `sqlite3` tool) outside their rule; decision 42 reads with `FIXTURE_DENY_LIST`, `GITHUB_ACTIONS` and `TMPDIR` as a test tool's variables, like the harness variables of decision 59; decision 71's shellcheck rule covers both scripts. Known limits, stated in the README: the CSV scan reads lines, not cells (a quoted field with a line break is read as two lines, and a phrase split across them is missed); the date exemption is by shape (`20260230` passes; an eight-digit real number shaped like a date is the deny list's job, and so is a date-shaped run inside an identifier, `AB20260102CD`); a run followed by a period and one to three digits passes even when it is an identifier (`1234567.001`), while a fractional part of seven or more digits fails (`0.1234567`); no Unicode normalization (a composed and a decomposed accent are different strings); a TEXT value that is itself JSON (milestone 3's `import_rows.raw`, `imports.header`, `import_profiles.config`, `rules.conditions`) is scanned as stored, so an escaped line break or tab glues the next word and a JSON number of seven or more digits inside it fails (milestone 3 slice 9 decides whether to decode such values); a virtual table, its shadow tables and a stored generated column are not read (a migration that adds one amends this row first); a CSV that is UTF-8 but for a stray byte is decoded from Windows-1252 throughout, so an accented entry is missed where the file spells it in UTF-8 (an ASCII entry is still found unless a non-ASCII character stands next to it); A password has at least 10 characters (Unicode scalar values) and at most 1,024 bytes, counted as typed, and no composition rule (5.3); it is never trimmed, and before it is hashed or verified it is normalized to NFKC, so that look-alike spellings of one letter (an accent typed as one character or as two, a full-width letter) make one password, as NIST advises, and a password typed on a phone matches the one typed on a laptop; a change of this rule after `v0.2.0` could stop a stored password with such letters from verifying until an admin resets it. The hash is argon2id from the `argon2` crate 0.6.0 with its default features, whose default parameters are 5.3's. The parameters are handed in by the caller instead of being switched by `cfg(test)`, which reaches neither nage-web's HTTP tests (integration tests link the library built without it) nor the binary, while a `test-support` feature may only add (59): the binary hands in m=19456 KiB, t=2, p=1, pinned by a test, and tests hand a cheap set, m=8 KiB, t=1, p=1, from `nage_core::test_support`. A stored hash names its own parameters, so it verifies whatever set is handed in; the set handed in makes new hashes and the dummy hash, which `serve` makes once with that set before it listens, so that no request waits for it and a set argon2 refuses stops the start as an error, never a panic, and which is verified against for an unknown username or a stored hash that cannot be read, so that a refused sign-in costs the same whatever its reason (5.3). Verification tells a wrong password from a stored hash it cannot read. On the server, hashing and verification run outside any database transaction, in a blocking thread of nage-web's hashing (5.3, 5.4), and at most four at once, a further one waiting for its turn, since each takes about 19 MiB, so that a burst of attempts cannot exhaust the server's memory; each command hashes before it opens its write transaction, in the order its doc comment fixes (5.4). The core's session operations take the write transaction, or a connection for a read, and the instant where time matters: create (`last_seen_at` equal to `created_at`, `expires_at` 30 days later, 92), find a valid session (its hash known, not yet expired, its user active), touch, delete the expired ones (at each sign-in, 106) and the write half of a sign-in (106) as one operation; delete one (sign-out), end every session of a user (an admin's reset, 112) and end all but one (one's own change, 112) take no instant. The cookie is `nage_session` with `Path=/`, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax` and a `Max-Age` of the session's expiry minus now in seconds, sent when the session is created and again whenever its expiry is rewritten (5.3's sliding refresh); it carries `Secure` exactly when trust-proxy is on and the first value of `X-Forwarded-Proto` is `https` (5.3), since Nage never inspects the connection and a browser drops a Secure cookie that arrives over plain HTTP, as on the LAN; sign-out clears the cookie with `Max-Age=0`, and so does the session rule (109) for a request whose cookie names no valid session (unknown, expired or a deactivated user's), on every answer it gives or lets through, unless the answer sets a new cookie, as an accepted sign-in does; the answers given before it, the body limit's 413 to a body that declares a length over 64 KiB and the Origin check's 403 (110), and its own error answers (503 for a busy database, 500 for any other failure) leave the cookie as it is; `/health` and `/static/*` never read the cookie. The touch (5.3: at most once an hour per session) happens only on a GET or HEAD whose session was last seen an hour ago or more, in a short write transaction of its own that rewrites `last_seen_at` and `expires_at` (the earlier of now plus 30 days and the creation plus 90, 92); when the writer does not come within a tenth of a second the touch is skipped and the page served, so a page view never waits behind a long write, and a mutating request never touches, so it stays one transaction (INV-28). nage-web reads no time of day: `serve` hands it a clock (113), and tests hand a clock they set, so expiry and the hourly touch are tested without waiting | The token made in nage-core (a second random source there, and tests that cannot choose the token); the token itself stored (5.3 stores only its hash); a touch on every request (a write per page view); a touch inside a mutating request's transaction (a refused request would leave it, INV-28); `Max-Age` sent on every response; a cookie crate (a dependency for a few lines) |

While no user exists, every request but `/setup`, `/health` and `/static/*` is sent to `/setup` (a 303, or for htmx as below), `/login` included (W1 step 1, 5.3); once a user exists the router remembers it (the `has_user` flag of its state), since users are never deleted, so a book set up by `nage init` while the server runs is seen at the next request. Otherwise a request without a valid session (108) answers 303 to `/login`, with `next` carrying the original request's path and query, percent-encoded, when the request is a GET for an address other than `/` that starts with exactly one `/`, holds no backslash or control character and is at most 2 KiB long, and with no `next` otherwise; after sign-in `next` is followed only when it passes the same test, and `/` takes its place when it does not or is absent, so it never leads off the site (5.3: never an absolute URL). An htmx request (`HX-Request: true`) without a session gets 401 with `HX-Redirect: /login` (`/setup` while no user exists), since htmx would follow a 303 itself and put the sign-in page inside the page; htmx's history-restore request, which ignores `HX-Redirect`, gets the 303. A request with a valid session reaches its route as the signed-in user; an unknown address then answers 404, and slice 6 gives that answer a page. The response event of 47 gains the signed-in user's id, none without a session (5.2); the typed username is never logged, and neither are the cookie and form bodies (5.2) | Unknown addresses answered 404 without a session (INV-27 covers every request); a 303 for htmx requests; `HX-Redirect` on htmx's history-restore request (htmx ignores it there); an absolute or unchecked `next`; asking the database at every request whether a user exists |

A failure is a checked attempt that misses: a wrong password at sign-in, an unknown username included (checked against the dummy hash, 107), a deactivated user whatever the password typed, so that a deactivated username behaves as an unknown one (106), and a wrong current password on the change-password page, which counts for the signed-in user's username (112; the maintainer's choice). For each username the server keeps the count of failures since the last success; the count is forgotten when 3,600 seconds have passed since that username's last failure, and a success forgets it at once: the right password of an active user at sign-in, or the right current password on the change-password page, even when the change is then refused for its new password. The first four failures start no pause; the fifth starts a pause of 60 seconds, the sixth one of 120 seconds, the seventh one of 300 seconds, and the eighth and every later one a pause of 600 seconds, each from the instant of the failure that starts it; a pause lasts the whole length it announced. This replaces 5.3's 2^(k−5) seconds and its 15-minute cap (the maintainer's choice): the waits are longer than 5.3's seconds, and five tries are free because a long password typed on a phone goes wrong easily. An attempt during a pause, at sign-in or on the change-password page, is refused with 429 and a `Retry-After` of the seconds left (the clock counts whole seconds), without checking the password and without counting as a failure; a later attempt is checked. So the sixth attempt is refused when it comes within a minute of the fifth failure (10.3's "engages on the sixth failure"). The pause is decided again when a verification ends, at sign-in and on the change-password page alike: an attempt whose verification ends during a pause for its username, begun meanwhile by another attempt's failure, gets the answer of an attempt made during that pause (429 with a `Retry-After` of the seconds left) whatever the verification found, is not counted as a failure and clears nothing; so attempts sent together are held to the same number of checked answers as attempts sent one after another, and of twelve wrong passwords sent together for a username with no failures yet, five are answered 422 and seven 429. A typed username that 95's normalization leaves longer than 64 characters is left unremembered only when it finds no user; one that finds a user (a username stored within 64 characters is found through a longer spelling too, such as each letter typed as a base and a combining mark, which the fold composes) has its misses counted like any other username's, however long the typed spelling is; and a username is dropped at once on a success and, once an hour has passed since its last failure, at the next failure of any username, so the memory holds only usernames with recent failures. A message is one box in two kinds, done (`role="status"`) and not done (`role="alert"`), each with a drawn sign, a tick or an exclamation mark, so that colour is not the only signal; a refused form marks each box that has a problem (`aria-invalid="true"`) besides the line under it, the help line keeping its own quiet colour; the set-up page's tick box takes the look and stays at least 44 by 44 with its help line below it; the busy page's way back looks like the other pages' single button; small signs stand before the menu's entries in both copies, a house before Home, a ledger before Accounts (137), a lock before Change password, a person before who is signed in and an arrow leaving a door before Sign out, each tied to its entry by a class of its own and never picked by the entry's address; the corner before an account inside another is drawn as a shape, since the fonts carry no box-drawing character and each engine would fall back to its own. The messages' signs and the menu's signs are drawn by the style file and read by no screen reader, the role already speaking for a message, and wherever the style file gives such content an empty alternative text, a plain declaration of the same content stands before it, so that an engine that does not know that form still shows the sign. There is no motion beyond a short fade and a button's press, in which the button moves 3 px onto its shadow; under `prefers-reduced-motion` nothing is animated, the press and the fade happening at once with no transition. Keyboard focus is clearly visible on every control; print is not designed. The light palette applies under `prefers-color-scheme: light` unless `data-theme="dark"` is set, and under `data-theme="light"` whatever the device says (124); a palette's block is written once per selector it needs, the device's setting and `data-theme`, and the blocks of one palette are equal name for name and value for value. Each block sets the `color-scheme` property of its palette, and every page's head carries `<meta name="color-scheme" content="dark light">`, so that a chosen Light on a dark device gets light form controls and scroll bars. Every colour is defined once per palette block as a named custom property and used by name; this row lists only colours that paint something, every named colour is used, and a new colour amends this row; where a role takes another listed colour in a palette, the style file names that colour, as the dark palette's main button's shadow and mark's shadow name the violet, so that palette has one violet. The named sizes include the tap size, the rail's width, the content and form columns, the radii and the outline widths, and in each palette block the offset of the Net worth block's shadow (0 in dark, 4 px in light), beside the others the style file names; other lengths may stand as literals, among them the 2.75rem height of a button and a box, which is the tap size on a phone and shrinks with a computer's smaller root. The 48rem switch point is a literal in each media query, since a media query cannot read a custom property, and a test holds its occurrences equal to each other and to the e2e suite's 768 px. A drawing that can be a mask painted in a named colour is one: the menu's signs, the ticked box's tick, the done message's tick, and the chevron (`--n-sign-next`) at the end of every line and choice that leads on and between the steps of the trail (130). Where a drawing cannot be one, the select's arrow and any background of the select itself, the colour inside its `data:` address equals the value this row lists for it in that palette, and a test finds such colours spelled with `#` or with `%23` and holds each equal to its named colour. Nage's mark (125) has two colours, the mark's navy `#10162f` on the accent `#ffd300`: both icon files, stand-alone drawings, fix both in their drawing, while `templates/mark.html`, drawn inline in every page, fixes only the navy and fills its tile with `currentColor`, which the style file paints in the accent by name; a test holds the colour literals of `templates/mark.html` and `static/icons/mark.svg` equal to the style file's ink on the accent and accent, and the template's tile painted in the accent, so the pair is recomputed from values held equal. The colours by role, dark then light: page `#0b1023`, `#f7f4ea`; panel `#10162f`, `#ffffff`; raised ground (hover, the entry in view in dark) `#18203f`, `#f1ede0`; panel outline `#2f3963`, `#10162f`; hair line between rows `#1f2850`, `#e6e2d4`; edge of form boxes and buttons `#8791c7`, `#10162f`; text `#f2f1ea`, `#10162f`; quiet text `#aeb4d0`, `#4a5073`; accent `#ffd300` in both; accent on hover `#ffe24d`, `#ffdf33`; ink on the accent `#10162f` in both; link `#ffd300`, `#3a10e5`; entry in view (ground, ink, bar) `#18203f`, `#ffd300`, `#ffd300` and `#ffd300`, `#10162f`, `#10162f`; done `#7fe0a7`, `#00693e`; not done `#ff8fa0`, `#be1809`; a message's sign on its block `#0b1023`, `#ffffff`; violet `#8b7dff`, `#3a10e5` (in the dark palette the main button's shadow and the mark's shadow, in the light one the colour of the link and the focus ring); focus ring `#ffd300`, `#3a10e5` (2 px, 3 px from the control; on a text box or the select, the box's edge turns the focus colour with a 1 px ring around it instead); the main button's shadow and edge, the violet and `#ffd300` in dark, `#10162f` and `#10162f` in light; other hard shadows `#3a4470`, `#10162f`; box ground `#0b1023`, `#fffdf7`; Net worth block (ground, ink, label, edge) `#18203f`, `#f2f1ea`, `#aeb4d0`, `#3a4470` and `#ffffff`, `#10162f`, `#4a5073`, `#10162f`; the mark's shadow, the violet in dark and `#10162f` in light. No text colour of the light palette is the accent, which a test holds. Contrast in both palettes, recomputed by a test from those named colours for every palette block: at least 4.5 to 1 for text on the page, a panel, a raised ground and a box ground; quiet text on the page, a panel and a raised ground; a link on the page and a panel; the entry in view's ink on its ground; ink on the accent; done on a panel; not done on a panel and the page; a message's sign on the done and the not-done block; the Net worth ink and label on its ground; and at least 3 to 1 (large text, the edges of boxes and buttons) for the edge on a panel, the page, a box ground and a raised ground; not done as a refused box's edge on a box ground; the focus ring on the page and a panel; the select's drawn arrow (quiet) on a box ground; a menu sign (text) on a panel and the entry in view's sign on its ground; the person sign (quiet) on a panel; and Nage's mark, its navy on its accent. Six owner colours (138), named n-owner-1 to n-owner-6 and used for an owner's pill on the account pages, dark then light: teal `#5fd4c8`, `#00756c`; violet `#b8a2ff`, `#6a3fd0`; blue `#7fb2ff`, `#1f5fc4`; green `#a9d46a`, `#36700f`; orange `#ffa868`, `#b04a00`; orchid `#f08cd2`, `#a3287f`; each is the pill's text and edge on a ground of the panel mixed with 14 percent of it in the dark palette and 8 percent in the light, at least 4.5 to 1, recomputed by a test | Pico's look with Nage's colours (each prototype spent a quarter to a third of its style file fighting it); the two other directions the maintainer saw on pictures, a terminal look and a soft one; a script framework such as Solid for the menu (it needs none, and 5.3 allows no script but htmx); one design for every width; yellow text on the light ground (1.31 to 1.44 to 1, below every rule); colour alone telling a done message from a refused one; the signs and the corner as characters of the font (Plex's Latin-1 files hold no tick and no box-drawing character); the signs picked by their entry's address (a new entry could silently lose its sign); every length a named size (a name for each margin would hide the few sizes that matter); the switch point as a custom property (a media query cannot read one); colours written into a drawing where a mask in a named colour would do (a palette change would leave them behind); a design for print in this slice |
| 124 | The appearance switch (clarifies 5.3's cookies and its policy, 108's Secure rule, 109's `next`, 110 and INV-27 for a new route, INV-28 for a route that writes nothing, and 116's menu; amends 110's list of layers) (provisional, maintainer to confirm) | The pages follow the device's light or dark setting (`prefers-color-scheme`), and a person can choose for the browser in use. In the menu of every signed-in page, in both copies, stands a group labelled Appearance with three buttons, Automatic, Light and Dark; the one in force carries `aria-pressed="true"` and the other two `aria-pressed="false"`. It is a plain form, with no script: a button posts the field `theme` (`automatic`, `light` or `dark`) and the field `back` to `POST /theme`. `back` holds the address of the page the form stands on, its path with its query as the request had them, escaped by the template like any text, so that after a choice the person stays on the page they were on (the maintainer's choice at the pause); on the page for an unknown address it holds `/`, since that page never prints its address (118). The route needs a valid session and passes the Origin check like every form (109, 110, INV-27); it accepts the three values only, exactly as written, and refuses any other readable value with 422 and a refusal page in 102's three parts under the 403's heading, This form was not accepted, with the 403's button, Back to Nage; what cannot be read as a form (the field `theme` named twice, another content type) gets the same bare answer the three older forms give. It keeps Light or Dark for that browser in a third cookie beside `nage_session` (108) and `nage_message` (117), `nage_theme`, which holds `light` or `dark` and never text, with `Path=/`, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax` as the session cookie has it, `Secure` by 108's rule and a lifetime of one year (`Max-Age=31536000`), while Automatic removes it (an empty value with `Max-Age=0` and the same attributes); and it answers 303 to `back` when 109's test for `next` accepts it as a path on this site, and to `/` otherwise. The route writes nothing to the database, and a refused, busy or failed request sets no appearance cookie, which is INV-28's no trace for it. Every page, the sign-in, set-up, refusal and busy pages included, tells the browser its palette by `data-theme="light"` or `data-theme="dark"` on its `html` element when the cookie holds one of those two values, and by no such attribute otherwise, so that the style file follows the device (123); the palette mark is never written from the cookie's text, and an unknown or missing value, like cleared cookies, means Automatic. A new layer, the appearance, reads the cookie for every request, so a refusal and the busy page wear the palette too; it stands between the security headers and the body limit, so 110's layers become six and run, outermost first: the request log (47), the security headers (40), the appearance, the body limit, the Origin check and the session rule (109). Like every cookie, this one is never logged (5.2). The policy and the other headers do not change. Automatic removes only the cookie Nage itself set (this host, `Path=/`): a `nage_theme` set for another path or for a parent domain stays and keeps deciding the palette and the pressed button until it is removed there, as 117 records for `nage_message`; whoever can set cookies for the host can force no more than light or dark, and this known limit is accepted. Nothing is ever written over a file: Nage creates the backup's file itself, new and private (mode 0600, 154), and only then lets VACUUM INTO, on the writer connection outside any transaction, write into that empty file, the path bound as a parameter and never assembled into the statement; a file already at the name, an empty one or a dangling symbolic link included, fails that creation and stops the update with nothing written and the file left as it is, and any other failed creation is an input-output error of its own. A second start within the same second meets Nage's own copy at the name: the message says that a file already carries the backup's name and that Nage never writes over a file, and its What now says to start Nage again in a moment, since the name carries the time to the second, and to move the file away only if Nage did not write it. The copy is then checked by a public function of its own, given the path and the step expected (SQLite's integrity check, the copy opened read-only, and its schema version, which must be the step its name carries); a backup that fails or does not pass is removed, and nothing is updated. Once it passed, every other copy of the same step (the same last change and the same app version) is removed, whatever its stamp, since a clock set back may leave a later stamp on an older copy, so a server restarting in a loop keeps one copy, not one per start (the maintainer's pick "Newest copy replaces the last"); a copy of another step and a file that is not a backup are never touched. ## 3. Goals

- G1. First evening: a household member goes from a fresh install to categorized books in one sitting. Setup, create accounts, build one import profile per bank, import a month, categorize, read the report.
- G2. Monthly upkeep under 30 minutes for about six accounts and 400 rows a month: re-import, clear the review queue, categorize the remainder, reconcile each account.
- G3. The books always balance. Every invariant in section 9 has an ID and a test; every invariant that can be checked after the fact also has a line in the output of `nage check`.
- G4. Nothing is lost by accident. Raw import rows are kept verbatim, deletes are soft, a backup is one button, export is complete. The two deliberate exceptions, undoing an import and merging the two sides of a transfer, remove exactly what they say.
- G5. Runs on modest hardware with no external services. One binary, one data directory, no Node toolchain needed to build. The published image is amd64; other targets build from source.
- G6. Daily tasks work on a phone: categorizing, entering a cash expense, checking a balance.
- G7. Household aware. Accounts have owners, transfers between members are transfers, and every change records who made it.

## 4. Non-goals

Each item names the reason and the earliest version it could land. None of these may be partially built in v1 "because it was easy".

| Non-goal | Why not now | Earliest |
|----------|-------------|----------|
| Multi-currency | Touches every report and invariant; the single biggest complexity multiplier. A USD column in a CAD export is never imported | v2 |
| Budgets | Only useful once categories have settled after months of real use | v2 |
| Investments with lots, prices, cost basis | A product of its own; balance-only accounts cover net worth | v3 |
| Direct bank connections (Plaid, SimpleFIN, GoCardless) | Credentials, API keys, rate limits, and a murky open-source story | v3 |
| OFX/QFX/QIF import | The pipeline after parsing is format-agnostic, so this is a parser away | v2 |
| Accrual accounting | Personal books are cash basis | never |
| Recurring and scheduled transactions | Imports bring in the real ones; templates need a posting engine and reminders | v2 |
| Receipts and attachments | File storage, thumbnails, bigger backups, no gain for the must-nail workflow. Uploaded CSVs are never stored | v2 |
| Per-user private accounts, roles beyond admin | Authorization filter on every query doubles the test surface | v2 |
| Expense splitting between members, who-owes-whom | The household is one economic unit; settlement tracking is a different product | never in core |
| Sync, offline, multi-device merge | Conflict resolution for financial data is a v3 problem | v3 |
| Multiple books per install | One household, one book; run two instances if needed | v2 |
| Stable public JSON API | htmx endpoints are internal and may change freely; `/health` promises only its HTTP status | v2 |
| Localization beyond currency minor digits | English UI only; one number format; the currency table holds codes and minor digits, nothing else | v2 |
| Native mobile apps | The responsive web UI is the phone app | never |
| Forecasting, bill reminders, invoicing, tax reports | Out of the household bookkeeping scope | v3 |
| Payee entities, merchant enrichment | Payee is text; rules and suggestions do the work | v2 |
| Plugin system | No stable internal API to plug into | v3 |
| TLS termination | Run behind tailscale serve, Caddy or nginx; documented, not built. Nage never inspects the connection for TLS | never |

## 5. Users, deployment, security

### 5.1 Users and the household

- One book, one household. Every signed-in user sees and may edit everything.
- The first user, created at setup, is the admin. Admins create users, reset passwords and deactivate users. The last remaining admin cannot be deactivated or demoted.
- Every user has a username (unique, case-insensitive), a display name and a password. No email, no self-service recovery; an admin resets passwords. Every user can change their own password and display name.
- Every account may have an owner (a user) or be joint. Ownership is a label for display and filtering. It is never an authorization boundary.
- Every row a user creates or edits records who and when, except sessions and postings; postings belong to their transaction, whose stamps cover them. The first admin's own `created_by` is empty; a user's `updated_by`/`updated_at` and `disabled_by`/`disabled_at` are empty until the first edit or deactivation and are then set together (decision 92). The `book` row carries no `created_by` at all: setup creates it before any user exists; its `updated_by` and `updated_at` arrive with the name edit (W14).

### 5.2 Deployment

- One binary `nage` with subcommands: `serve`, `health`, `init`, `user add`, `user reset-password`, `check`, `backup`, `export`, `migrate`. Development tooling (seeding scenario databases, generating fixtures, writing the currency table, decision 87) lives in an `xtask` crate, not in the shipped binary.
- Configuration via flags or `NAGE_*` environment variables (flag over variable over default; an empty variable counts as unset): data directory (`NAGE_DATA_DIR`, default `./data`, created with its `backups/` subfolder if missing), bind address (`NAGE_BIND`, default `127.0.0.1:8787`; the image sets `0.0.0.0:8787`), trust-proxy flag (`NAGE_TRUST_PROXY`, default off), time zone (`NAGE_TZ`, an IANA name; defaults to the `TZ` variable, then UTC; an invalid name in whichever variable is used refuses to start), log filter (`NAGE_LOG`, a tracing filter directive, default `info`, text format).
- Logs carry method, path without query string, status, latency and user id. Never cookies, form bodies, passwords or CSV contents.
- Data directory holds `nage.sqlite` with its `-wal` and `-shm` companions, and `backups/`. Copying `nage.sqlite` alone while the server runs is not a backup; a backup is a `VACUUM INTO` snapshot (`nage backup`, the Settings button, or the host's consistent copy by path), or a copy of all three files with the server stopped.
- `GET /health` is unauthenticated and returns JSON with `version` and `ok`. The probe runs `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM book` on the read pool with a one-second deadline: status 200 whenever the query succeeds, whatever the count (a fresh database with no book row is healthy), 503 on an error or a timeout. Only the status code is a contract. `nage health` performs that request against the local server (derived from `NAGE_BIND`, with `0.0.0.0` mapped to `127.0.0.1` and `::` to `::1`), with a two-second timeout, exiting 0 only on 200 and 1 for any other outcome, except that an invalid or port-0 bind address is a configuration error and exits 2 (decision 44), so a container health check needs no curl.
- `serve` takes the data folder's in-use mark, applies pending migrations at startup, all or nothing, and logs how many it applied and the schema version, writing a pre-migration backup first (section 5.5; decisions 151 to 155), then runs `check` before it listens and logs the result, each broken rule at ERROR while the server serves as usual (decision 160). `check` on a database with no book row says that no book is set up yet and exits 0 (decision 97). `nage migrate [--data-dir DIR] [--dry-run]` does the same update with the server stopped and refuses while a server runs on the folder (decisions 153 and 156). Everything Nage creates in the data folder is private to the account Nage runs as, folders 0700 and files 0600 (decision 154). A log line is one event with a whole-second UTC stamp, the level, the message and key=value fields, with no module name (decision 160); a refusal or failure at the terminal is three labelled lines, a result a sentence with its facts (decisions 157 to 159). The server shuts down gracefully on SIGTERM and SIGINT.
- Published as an amd64 image on GHCR with exact version tags only (no `latest`), plus the same Linux binary attached to each GitHub release. The binary is a static `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl` build with SQLite bundled and the time-zone database embedded, so it runs on any x86_64 Linux and in a distroless runtime image. The image runs as uid 1000 with `/data` pre-created and owned by that uid, declares an exec-form `HEALTHCHECK` on `nage health`, and sets `NAGE_DATA_DIR=/data` and `NAGE_BIND=0.0.0.0:8787`.
- Versioning: each milestone PR bumps the workspace version to `0.N.0` (milestone 1's `0.1.0` was set in slice 1, decision 81); after the merge the maintainer pushes the tag `v0.N.0`, which runs the release workflow from milestone 2 on (`v0.1.0` has none to run, decision 81); the workflow fails if the tag and the Cargo version differ. Fix releases are `v0.N.x` from `fix/<slug>` PRs (decision 30).
- Reference deployment: the household server, an x86_64 Ubuntu box where every service is a Docker Compose project with a `data/` folder, a container user of 1000:1000, `restart: unless-stopped`, `TZ` set, and one published port reachable from the LAN and the tailnet. Nage follows that pattern. HTTPS for phones comes from `tailscale serve` in front of the published port; the LAN reaches it over plain HTTP. The host's nightly backup copies the data directory as files and takes a consistent copy of the SQLite file by path. Host-specific values (addresses, names, script paths) stay in that server's runbooks, never in this repo. `/setup` is open to anyone who can reach the port until it has been used, so the deployment guide recommends `nage init` before publishing the port.

### 5.3 Security

- Passwords hashed with argon2id at m=19456 KiB, t=2, p=1 (the OWASP recommendation and the argon2 crate default), on the server in a blocking thread outside any database transaction and by the commands outside any transaction; the binary hands these parameters in, and tests hand a cheap set (decision 107). Minimum length 10 characters, maximum 1 KiB, counted as typed, never trimmed and normalized to NFKC before hashing, no composition rules (decision 107); the setup and change-password forms ask for the password twice, and the change-password form asks for the current one first (decision 112). Login runs a dummy verification for unknown usernames so timing does not reveal them.
- Sessions: 32 random bytes; only the SHA-256 of the token is stored. Cookie `nage_session`, `Path=/`, HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax, with `Max-Age` refreshed as the session slides. It is marked Secure if and only if the trust-proxy flag is on and the request carries `X-Forwarded-Proto: https` (set by `tailscale serve` or a reverse proxy); Nage never inspects the connection itself. Expiry is the earlier of 30 days after last use and 90 days after creation; `last_seen_at` is written at most once an hour per session. Logout is a POST. A password reset revokes the user's sessions; changing one's own password revokes the user's other sessions (decision 112). Expired sessions are deleted at each login. Nage sets two more cookies, never read as a session: `nage_message`, a fixed code for a one-time message (decision 117), and `nage_theme`, `light` or `dark`, the appearance a person chose for the browser (decision 124), with `Path=/`, HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax, Secure by the session cookie's rule and a lifetime of one year; choosing Automatic removes it.
- Every route except `/setup`, `/login`, `/health` and `/static/*` requires a valid session for an active user. With no user in the database every other route redirects to `/setup`, `/login` included; `/setup` returns 404 once any user exists. An unauthenticated plain request gets a 303 to `/login?next=<relative path>` (never an absolute URL); an unauthenticated htmx request gets a 401 with `HX-Redirect: /login`, except htmx's history-restore request, which gets the 303 (decision 109).
- Every request but GET, HEAD, OPTIONS and TRACE (POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE, QUERY and any extension method) must carry an `Origin` header, or `Referer` when `Origin` is absent, whose host and port equal the request's `Host` header or, with trust-proxy on, its `X-Forwarded-Host`; the scheme is ignored. A missing or mismatched header gets 403. `Referrer-Policy: same-origin` keeps browsers from sending `Origin: null`.
- Response headers on every response: `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'self'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'; img-src 'self' data:; frame-ancestors 'none'; form-action 'self'; base-uri 'self'`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: same-origin`, and `Cache-Control: no-store` on every response that carries no `Cache-Control` of its own, a served asset keeping decision 46's (decision 119). The policy stands as it is: the fonts and images Nage serves itself are already allowed, a font is never a `data:` address and an image may be (decision 126). No inline scripts or styles anywhere; htmx is configured through its `<meta>` config with `allowEval: false` and `includeIndicatorStyles: false`, and `hx-on` attributes are not used.
- Login rate limit, kept in memory and keyed by the case-folded username whether or not it exists: from the fifth failure on, an attempt made while a lock lasts is refused with 429 and a `Retry-After`, the fifth failure starting a lock of 60 seconds, the sixth one of 120 seconds, the seventh one of 300 seconds and the eighth and every later one a lock of 600 seconds, each lasting its whole length; the count of failures is forgotten an hour after the username's last failure and reset on success. An attempt made while a lock lasts is refused without checking the password; an attempt whose check ends after a lock for its username has begun is refused with 429 and a `Retry-After` as well, whatever the check found, and neither counts as a failure nor resets the count. A wrong current password on the change-password form counts as a failure for the user's username (decision 111).
- Request bodies are limited to 64 KiB, except upload routes, which accept 10 MiB.
- No secrets in the database beyond password and session hashes. No telemetry, no outbound network calls of any kind.

### 5.4 Concurrency and HTTP conventions

- SQLite in WAL mode with `synchronous=NORMAL`, `foreign_keys=ON` and a busy timeout of five seconds. Writes go through a pool of exactly one connection and every write transaction starts with `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`; reads use a separate pool. A request queues up to 30 seconds for the single writer connection and up to 5 seconds for a reader. Password hashing and CSV parsing happen before the write transaction opens. When a queue wait expires, or SQLite answers SQLITE_BUSY after the busy timeout, the request gets 503 with `Retry-After: 5` and a "busy, try again" page.
- Every mutating request runs inside one SQLite transaction. An import, its sweep included, is one transaction; a partially written import cannot exist.
- Transactions (the ledger kind) carry a version number. Every write to a transaction or its postings, by a form, an import link, a merge, a reconciliation tick or a rule application, increments it by one. Only edit forms name the version they expect; a mismatch returns 409 and shows the current data. Two household members editing the same transaction cannot silently overwrite each other.
- Full-page forms are plain POSTs: 303 on success, 422 with the re-rendered form on validation errors, 409 with the current data on a stale version, 403 on a failed Origin check. htmx partial requests get the same codes, and htmx's response handling is configured to swap 409 and 422 bodies. Timestamps are written only from Rust as RFC 3339 UTC with whole seconds (`2026-10-26T12:00:00Z`), never from SQLite defaults, and displayed in the configured time zone. sqlx's own `_sqlx_migrations` table is exempt from this rule: it keeps its default timestamp, and Nage only reads the table. It is no longer exempt from the `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` rule above: every pending migration runs inside one outer `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, sqlx nesting each in a savepoint, so an update is all or nothing (decisions 57 and 155).

### 5.5 Migrations and the pre-migration backup

- sqlx migrations, forward only, applied by `serve` at startup and by `nage migrate` (with the server stopped). Migration files are frozen once released: enum-valued columns carry their full v1 value set in the migration that creates them, and a column's constraints are final from the column's creation.
- From milestone 2, when migrations are pending and the database already has a migrations table that holds a row, `serve` or `nage migrate` first writes `backups/<UTC stamp>-pre-migrate-<last applied migration>-to-<app version>.sqlite` (the stamp `YYYY-MM-DDTHH-MM-SSZ`, the migration as four digits) via `VACUUM INTO` into a file Nage creates new and private, then checks the copy (SQLite's integrity check and its schema version); a failed or unverified backup aborts the update without migrating, and a file already at the name, or at the name with SQLite's `-journal`, `-wal` or `-shm` after it (which SQLite would clear beside the new copy), stops it and is left as it is (decision 151). The very first migration gets no backup. Once the new copy passed, every other copy of the same step (the same last migration and app version) is removed, so retention is one copy per update step; the deployment guide says when to prune the rest. Every pending migration is then applied all or nothing (decision 155).
- An older binary started against a newer database exits 1 with a message saying a newer Nage wrote it and naming the newest backup it can open, or that none was found; the deployment guide describes the restore (decision 152).

## 6. Accounting model

### 6.1 Double entry, plain language

A transaction is a dated set of two or more postings. Each posting names an account and a signed integer amount in minor units. The amounts of a transaction's postings sum to zero. That is the whole model; everything else is presentation.

The UI never shows debits or credits. A simple transaction is shown as date, payee, amount, from-account, to-account. A split transaction is shown as one from-account and several to-lines whose amounts sum to the total. Internally these are just postings.

### 6.2 Chart of accounts

Five root accounts exist from setup, one per kind: Assets, Liabilities, Equity, Income, Expenses. Every other account has a parent and inherits its kind from its root. Categories are simply accounts under Income or Expenses. Full names join the path with a colon, for example `Expenses:Food:Groceries`.

Rules:
- Roots never receive postings. Any non-root account may.
- A name is normalized (trimmed, inner runs of white space collapsed to one space, control characters refused; decision 95), non-empty, at most 64 characters, and contains no colon. Sibling names are unique ignoring case (a Unicode-folded copy of the name is stored for the index); root names likewise. Depth is at most six path segments, the root included.
